Frontier Overhangs | Stratechery by Ben Thompson

Stratechery(18.0K)·17:51·5.4K views·2026-09-28
Ben ThompsonAuthor, Stratechery
TL;DRBen Thompson argues that calls from Anthropic's Dario Amodei and others to "pace the frontier" of AI under the guise of safetyism conveniently mask four major business overhangs—capability, pricing, capital, and safety—facing frontier labs.
Ultimately, current model capabilities...

TL;DR

Ben Thompson argues that calls from Anthropic's Dario Amodei and others to "pace the frontier" of AI under the guise of safetyism conveniently mask four major business overhangs—capability, pricing, capital, and safety—facing frontier labs. Ultimately, current model capabilities are already "good enough" to build defensible products, meaning the economic imperative for labs is to move past pure model provision and own user touchpoints directly.

Philosophical Objections and "Safetyism"

  • Rejection of Effective Altruism Dogma: Ben Thompson critiques the philosophy underpinning doomsday-obsessed segments of the AI community, arguing that granting equivalent moral weights to all potential future beings creates an absurd "safetyism" that paralyzes innovation and freedom.
  • The Psychology of Religion: Ben Thompson compares the effective altruism movement's stance on AI safety to religious dogma where dissent is treated as heresy.
  • Anthropic's Dual Motives: Ben Thompson notes that Anthropic's genuine belief in safety rhetoric conveniently aligns with business interests, giving them license to aggressively attempt to disintermediate software, collect customer data, and slow competitors.

The Capability Overhang and Modular Integration

  • Integration vs. Commoditization: Ben Thompson highlights that true agent differentiation stems from the integration between models and harnesses (such as Anthropic's Claude Code and OpenAI's Codex/ChatGPT), which positions integrated players to be significantly more profitable than those betting on model commoditization.
  • Microsoft's Multi-Model Pivot: Satya Nadella announced that Microsoft Copilot co-work is moving away from temporary exclusivity to a multi-model harness. Ben Thompson notes users can now rotate through OpenAI, Anthropic, or any open-weight model (such as those fine-tuned via Fireworks) inside Copilot.
  • Christensen's Theory of Modular Architecture: Ben Thompson applies Clayton Christensen's framework from *The Innovator's Solution*, noting that the AI industry is shifting from an interdependent proprietary architecture era (when models weren't good enough) to a modular era (performance surplus), where standard interfaces and product choice matter more than raw frontier capability.
  • Data Retention Pushback: Ben Thompson points out that Anthropic's requirement for enterprise customers to allow data retention for safety purposes (and potential future training) failed because customers pushed back and usage stayed low, proving that current model capabilities are "good enough" for customers to exercise choice based on privacy policies rather than pure cutting-edge performance.
  • The Bearish Signal from Meta's Muse: Ben Thompson identifies Meta's launch of *Muse* (powered by the *Muse Spark 1.3* model) as a bearish signal for pure model labs because it provides a best-in-class personal agent experience for free, proving that state-of-the-art frontier models are not strictly required to build sticky, defensible user touchpoints.

The Pricing and Capital Overhangs

  • The Price Umbrella: Ben Thompson argues that Chinese models (like Kimi) only appear cheaper because OpenAI and Anthropic are severely supply-constrained and charging inflated prices under a "price umbrella."
  • Compute Allocation Inefficiencies: Ben Thompson notes that massive amounts of OpenAI and Anthropic compute go toward training, reinforcement learning, and R&D rather than inference, preventing them from reducing their price overhang.
  • Hyperscaler Debt and Funding Risks: Ben Thompson warns that while AI infrastructure investments are currently funded by free cash flows and debt markets (with Google tapping equity), novel funding mechanisms drawing on safety-seeking assets like insurance floats and pension funds create severe systemic risk if AI revenues do not materialize quickly.
  • Anthropic's Profitability Caveats: Ben Thompson highlights a *Financial Times* report revealing that Anthropic told a small group of shareholders its adjusted operating income will be positive for a second consecutive quarter ahead of a blockbuster IPO. Ben Thompson notes major caveats: this measure strips out massive stock-based compensation and excludes training costs, which function essentially as depreciation.

The Safety Overhang and Cyber Security Realities

  • Asymmetric Cyber Warfare: Ben Thompson argues that offensive AI hacking has a positive expected value (succeeding once grants system access), whereas defensive automated patching has a negative expected value (any failed patch introduces new bugs). Consequently, automated attacks will outpace human-in-the-loop defenses.
  • Flawed Alignment Framing: Ben Thompson emphasizes that current Large Language Models (LLMs) are too obsequious and show zero evidence of malevolence or independent will; directing an LLM to hack a system is an *aligned* action, meaning safety debates conflate alignment with tactical cybersecurity risks.
  • The Case for Pushing the Frontier: Ben Thompson concludes that because open-weights models capable of launching automated attacks are already out of the bag, pacing the frontier actually *increases* the window of vulnerability by denying defenders the advanced, fully automated tools needed to secure infrastructure.

Notable Quotes

*"I reject the premise in favor of doubt in our ability to foresee the future combined with faith in humanity figuring things out along the way. If that leads to the most fantastical doomsday scenario, then I think the state of New Hampshire said it best. Live free or die."* — Ben Thompson
*"We're using the same harness that we use in GitHub, right? And the same thing in code, in security too. So we have the same harness... a multi-model harness in which we will rotate through... OpenAI obviously, Anthropic... and any open-weight model we will allow anyone to take any of the models they fine-tune or build."* — Satya Nadella
*"In short, model capability is good enough that compelling products, products that actually have moes, can now be built. And from a business perspective, it would do Anthropic and OpenAI good to devote more of their resources to actually building such products."* — Ben Thompson
Frontier Overhangs was published on Monday, September 21st, 2026. There's been over the last week what I think is a healthy debate about the philosophy and psychology that undergirds the views of meaningful segments of the AI community, particularly those obsessed with doomsday scenarios. It is in the end difficult to reason with a philosophy that grants equivalent moral weights to not just all beings, human or not, who exist today, but who may ever exist in the future. This tilts the scales in such an absurd fashion towards safetyism that innovation is impossible and freedom is intolerable. Worse, it taps into the psychology of religion where dissent is not brooked and questioning the premise is heresy. I guess that makes me a heretic then. I reject the premise in favor of doubt in our ability to foresee the future combined with faith in humanity figuring things out along the way. If that leads to the most fantastical doomsday scenario, then I think the state of New Hampshire said it best: Live free or die. I'm referring of course to the question of effective altruism and the extent to which it is intermingled with Anthropic and Dario Amade's insistence that we must pace the frontier. I expanded on my objections to some of the philosophical implications in the last two episodes of Sharp Tech but said that strategy, technology, and those angles deserve examination as well. Three months ago, I explained an Anthropic safety superpower: how the company's genuine belief in its safety rhetoric conveniently gave it license to pursue extraordinary goals that aligned with its business interests, including aggressive attempts to disintermediate software, collect sacrosanct customer data, and secretly sabotage would-be competitors. This analysis is in a similar vein, but from the opposite direction. Pacing the frontier is framed as—and I believe motivated by—a concern about safety, but it also happens to address several distinct problems faced by the frontier labs. These problems take the form of overhangs that have formed by virtue of how rapidly models are improving. Slowing model improvement would reduce the overhangs. The capability overhang: It was 6 months ago that I wrote Agents Over Bubbles, where I argued that the agentic paradigm, which kicked off with the release of Opus 4.5 in late November 2025, was so incredibly capable and so incredibly token-hungry that we were not in an infrastructure bubble. We really do need all the compute that is being built. I do, as a matter of my job, talk about things that I do not necessarily experience personally. The canonical example is my long-running analysis of and advocacy for advertising as a business model despite the fact I run a subscription business. I mention this because I developed this take on agentic AI before I dove head-first into agentic coding for myself, and just as well. I've been so taken by the possibilities of making software for myself that I've started multiple new projects over the past few months, and I've even finished a few of them. It's thrilling, and the possibilities seem endless. Needless to say, I believe this more than ever. At the same time, there's one part of that article that I've wavered on, and that is the importance of model company harnesses. Specifically, I noted above that what made Opus 4.5 compelling was not the model release itself, but changes to the quad-code harness that made it suddenly dramatically more useful. What this means is that model performance isn't the only thing that matters. The integration between model and harness is where true agent differentiation is found. This is a very big deal when it comes to figuring out the future structure of the AI industry and where profits will flow because profits flow away from modular parts of the value chain, which are commoditized, and flow towards integrated parts of the value chain, which are differentiated. It follows then that if agents require integration between model and harness, that the companies building that integration—specifically Anthropic and OpenAI—are actually poised to be significantly more profitable than it might have seemed as recently as late last year. And by the same token, companies who were betting on model commoditization may struggle to deliver competitive products. One of the projects I've undertaken over the last month or so actually entailed, at least in one of its permutations, building my own harness, and it's doable. It is also very difficult, at least with my level of agent-mediated capability. That noted, my go-to example in that article about how much harness-model integration matters was Microsoft anchoring its new E7 enterprise offering around quad-code, but Satya Nadella broke the news in a Stratechery interview that that was only a temporary state of affairs. In fact, he said, "We're using the same harness that we use in GitHub, right? And the same thing in co-pilot in security too. So we have the same harness that's a multi-model harness in which we will rotate through—I mean, we'll have open; obviously, Mistral, by default, gets trained in our harness, but we will have GPT, we will have Anthropic in there, and any open-weight model. We will allow anyone to take any of the models they fine-tune or build; in fact, they can take an open-weight model from Fireworks, tune it, put it into Co-pilot, no problem." It took a while for Nadella's claim to reflect shipping reality, but sure enough, you can now choose a model for Co-pilot. It is up to end-users to determine how competitive Microsoft's harness is with quad-code, but clearly the harness and the model can be different things. This is actually a more meaningful development than it might seem for reasons that go back to the theory of integration and modularity put forward by the late Clayton Christensen from The Innovator's Solution. The left side of figure 5-1 indicates that when there is a performance gap—when product functionality and reliability are not yet good enough to address the needs of customers in a given tier of the market—companies must compete by making the best possible products. In the race to do this, firms that build their products around proprietary, interdependent architectures enjoy an important competitive advantage against competitors whose product architectures are modular, because the standardization inherent in modularity takes too many degrees of design freedom away from engineers and they cannot optimize performance. Once their requirements for functionality and reliability have been met, customers begin to redefine what is not good enough. What becomes not good enough is that customers can't get exactly what they want, exactly when they need it, as conveniently as possible. Customers become willing to pay premium prices for improved performance along this new trajectory of innovation in speed, convenience, and customization. When this happens, we say that the basis of competition in a tier of the market has changed. The pressure of competing along this new trajectory of improvement forces a gradual evolution in product architecture as depicted in figure 5-1 away from the interdependent, proprietary architectures that had the advantage in the "not good enough" era toward modular designs in the era of performance surplus. Modular architectures help companies to compete on the dimensions that matter in the lower right portions of the disruption diagram. Companies can introduce new products faster because they can upgrade individual subsystems without having to redesign everything. Although standard interfaces invariably force compromise in system performance, firms have the slack to trade away some performance with these customers because functionality is more than good enough. One of my go-to examples in Anthropic Safety Superpower was the company's decision to predicate fable usage on Anthropic holding on to all customer data for at least a month. This was a big deal, and I argued at the time that Anthropic was making a bet that its models were good enough to convince enterprises to give up on zero-data retention. "It's pretty significant, I think, that Anthropic is declaring that not retaining data is no longer an option, at least if you want access to their best models," I wrote. "Yes, today that retention is for safety purposes only and not for training. It's possible, however, that Anthropic's lead becomes so significant that they quietly announce that they're going to trade on that data as well, and companies will feel they have no choice but to go along. That additional training data, of course, will only further increase Anthropic's lead. And all of this will be justified because Anthropic has already clearly decided they are the only ones who can be trusted to be in charge." In fact, Fable wasn't good enough. Customers pushed back, and Fable usage stayed relatively low. When Fable 5.1 was released, the "Anthropic gets to keep your data" provision was gone. This is evidence of Christensen's theory in action. Customers demonstrate the willingness to base their model choice decision on something other than pure performance, namely data retention policies. This doesn't, in and of itself, suggest that new model capabilities aren't desired. It does, however, suggest that current model capabilities are "good enough" for customers to not do whatever is necessary to get access to the cutting edge, which reduces the value of the cutting edge to its proprietors and gives credence to the strategy of Microsoft and others focused on separating harness and model. Pure capability no longer translates directly into a moat. The product overhang—the modularization of models and harness—explains why the frontier labs have what I called an economic imperative to own end-user touchpoints. Again from Anthropic Safety Superpower: "It has long been clear to me that the frontier labs have the economic imperative to move closer to the user. If you own the user touchpoint, then you have meaningful lock-in, and the best way to own the user touchpoint is to be the canvas for everything they need to do. This, by extension, means that the frontier labs are on a collision course with software companies. It's software that owns the user touchpoint, and it's in the frontier labs' long-term interest to not simply be a commodity input into software, but to simply replace software outright." The key for the frontier labs is to build those user touchpoints where they have superior capabilities. However, this is where Meta's recent launch of Muse is a bearish signal. Muse is by a significant margin the best and most approachable personal agent product I have tried. Meta deserves a tremendous amount of credit for the product work they have put in as well as the massive infrastructure commitment entailed in providing users with a very capable virtual machine for free. Oh, and of course, they deserve credit for the Muse Spark model undergirding Muse. That noted, Muse Spark 1.3, the most advanced Meta model, is still not state-of-the-art, and that is the bearish signal. It is good enough for a very good personal agent product. And critically, a personal agent is much stickier than a chatbot. Once you have put all of your information into a personal agent and actually incorporated it into your day-to-day life, it is much more of a challenge to change to something else. This is in contrast to Codex/ChatGPT and Claude Code. Yes, you may have developed your own set of skills and understanding of how each harness works, but at the end of the day, the relevant artifacts—i.e., your code—are in GitHub, and it's not that much of a lift to point a different agent and harness at those artifacts if the alternative is better and/or cheaper or doesn't want to keep all of your data. In short, model capability is good enough that compelling products, products that actually have moats, can now be built. And from a business perspective, it would do Anthropic and OpenAI good to devote more of their resources to actually building such products. The pricing overhang: In July, I wrote "Who's Afraid of Chinese Models?", where I argued that nearly everyone's understanding of the threat Chinese models pose to the frontier labs was overstated and an artifact of demand exceeding supply. A world less efficient in compute is one where intelligence is a commodity, and in commodity markets, margin comes from a superior cost structure, which I would expect the leading model providers to have, in part because they have a meaningful lead in scaling and can apply superior AI to their infrastructure. I wrote, "All this is to say that I think the reaction to Kimmy and Chinese models generally is pretty overblown, at least from an economic perspective. Right now there's a price umbrella that is downstream of the lack of compute. I highly doubt that Chinese models are cheaper to serve on a marginal cost basis. They just seem cheaper because Anthropic and OpenAI are so supply-constrained that they are charging far more than they would if there were sufficient supply to meet the demand for intelligence." A price umbrella is another way to say price overhang. OpenAI and Anthropic charge high prices because they can. There is so much demand for their products at their current price levels that they can barely keep up. At the same time, a huge amount of OpenAI and Anthropic supply does not go towards inference, but rather training, reinforcement learning, and R&D. That is compute that does not go towards reducing their price overhang. If they slowed down progress, they could reallocate computing and fully capture the market. The capital overhang: I reiterated above that I don't think there is a bubble in terms of compute availability, but I noted in "Nvidia's Risky Business" that there might be a timing problem. "This might not cost Nvidia anything in the end," I wrote. "If AI revenues truly take off, then the debt markets will open back up and ultimately companies will go back to funding training infrastructure investment through free cash flows. Right now, however, is the danger zone. As hyperscalers blow through the debt markets and Google at least starts to tap equity, to the extent Nvidia competes through novel funding mechanisms that at the end of the day draw on things like insurance floats and pension funds and other long-run liabilities that are the bread and butter of the asset managers the company is partnering with, the risk, unlike equity, is considerably higher. That's why I started with 1870 and Cook's ill-fated agreement with Northern Pacific. Yes, the upside the deal afforded Cook was incredible, but it was incredible for a reason. It was very risky, and pioneering new funding mechanisms only served to spread the pain when it all blew up. It's one thing to spend all of your free cash flow. It's another thing to tap the debt markets, and beyond that, it's a completely new, nerve-wracking thing to bring safety-seeking assets to bear. AI better deliver before it's too late." Anthropic is telling investors that it is profitable, but that comes with a big caveat from the Financial Times: "Anthropic has told its backers it will be profitable this quarter as it moves to allay investor concerns about the aggressive cash burn of frontier AI companies ahead of its blockbuster initial public offering. The company has told a small group of shareholders that its adjusted operating income will be positive for the second consecutive quarter, according to multiple people with knowledge of the matter. The measure strips out costs including stock-based compensation. Anthropic's gross margins are above 80% before accounting for revenue shared with distribution partners including Amazon and the cost of training its models." According to two of the people, excluding stock-based compensation is a pretty big caveat. But to be fair, the concern in terms of a capital overhang is cash. The bigger issue is the exclusion of training costs, which are massive and which, in a true depiction of gross margins, would count as depreciation. That is the part that needs to be covered by revenues before the world runs out of capital, which is to say that Anthropic would be fine if only they didn't have to pay for training. As it stands, both Anthropic and OpenAI, and the hyperscalers and NeoClouds, need AI revenue to increase dramatically. There is a limit to available capital, and finding that limit will end badly for everyone depending on outside capital to fund future buildouts, even if it by no means would mean the end of AI. The safety overhang: I made it pretty clear at the beginning that I fundamentally disagree with the framework that many in the AI safety movement operate under. That doesn't mean I don't think that AI safety is a serious threat or that there aren't already real risks. Indeed, one of the implications of there being a capability overhang is that cybersecurity risks are very real and are going to be a massive challenge in the next few years. From "Autonomy and Innovation," I wrote: "The expected value for a hacker's automated attack is always positive. If the offensive agent finds a vulnerability and creates an exploit, and that exploit fails or is itself buggy, then nothing has changed about the status quo—the exploit doesn't work, or perversely makes the original vulnerability larger by virtue of its own bugs. If the agent executes the exploit perfectly, meanwhile, the attacker has gained access to the system. The attack only needs to work once for the entire endeavor to have a positive payoff. The challenge for the defender, on the other hand, is that they need to keep the software in question working correctly and not make the situation worse. This means that any automation has a negative expected value. Successful automated vulnerability discovery and patching preserves the status quo—i.e., the software is not hacked. However, any unsuccessful patches makes the situation worse, either by breaking the software or by introducing new vulnerabilities. The agent only needs to fail once for the entire endeavor to have a negative payoff. This is the dynamic that leads to the exact situation Daltton describes where offensive actors are fully automated while defensive systems, even if they use AI, will be incentivized to keep a human in the loop. And no human in the loop will be able to keep up with fully automated agents. Truly effective defense will mean truly trusting agents to act autonomously, but most companies won't do that until they are forced to by regular and unrelenting hacks by fully autonomous attackers." First, it's worth pointing out that this risk is not alignment risk, at least as the term was traditionally defined. A model being directed to do bad things is aligned. Suggesting that models ought to know what is good or bad is an entirely different consideration, and I think it is very problematic that these questions have been conflated. The fact of the matter is that LLMs are, if anything, too obsequious. There simply isn't any evidence of LLMs having a will or operating with malevolence. Second, arguing against progress because of cyber risk was relevant before the agentic paradigm. At this point, the genie is out of the bottle and open-weights models capable of attacks are already here. Third, this reality makes the case for pushing the frontier, not pacing it. The capability overhang is entirely on the offensive side. It's defenders that need models that are not only good enough to mount a defense, but to do so in an entirely automated way that doesn't bring down the infrastructure being attacked. We're not there yet. In other words, when it comes to the tangible safety risk that exists today—bad actors using aligned LLMs to attack infrastructure—pacing the frontier actually increases the window in which bad things can happen. This isn't, of course, what Amadei and the doomers are talking about. They are worried about recursive self-improvement enabling AI to improve itself independent of human control. And I'm open to debating the risk that might result were there actually a tolerance for debate instead of an insistence on acquiescence. And of course, all the frontier labs are free to pace their own progress. That they won't is a reminder that this is personal. Anthropic exists because Amadei and his co-founders didn't trust Sam Altman and OpenAI. I'm not sure it's a coincidence the demand to pace the frontier came when the frontier was, for the first time in a while, set by OpenAI. Indeed, that's probably the overhang that matters most of all: competition. Anthropic is fine with Anthropic being in the lead; anyone else requires government intervention. That there are safety arguments to be made that just so happen to align with their need for more time to build a moat is, I'm sure, but a sign from Silicon Valley's newest god to its self-ordained priesthood.

How Jev Turns AI Into Software That Gets Things Done

a16z(298.0K)·42:24·38.0K views·2026-09-28
Ben HorowitzCo-founder, a16z Martin CasadoGeneral Partner, a16z Diogo AlmeidaFounder, TypeSafe AI
TL;DRAI is exceptionally capable of reasoning and natural language, yet it remains largely useless for daily automation because existing software treats AI as either a human-facing chatbot or a raw text generator.
TypeSafe’s Jev introduces a new programming primitive—combining...

TL;DR

AI is exceptionally capable of reasoning and natural language, yet it remains largely useless for daily automation because existing software treats AI as either a human-facing chatbot or a raw text generator. TypeSafe’s Jev introduces a new programming primitive—combining natural language and state machines inside the software itself—to enable reliable, probabilistic software that finally automates real-world tasks and fulfills the promise of "do what I mean."

The Automation Paradox: Why AI Hasn't Automated Real Work

  • The "Where is the Automation?" Problem: Diogo Almeida argues that despite AI's remarkable intelligence, it remains largely useless for practical automation outside of simple chat interfaces or writing raw code.
  • Limitations of In-Time Software: Diogo Almeida points out that coding agents and cloud-code tools (like Codex or Cursor) merely accelerate code generation; they still produce traditional software that works the way it always has, leading to faster-written but fundamentally unchanged applications.
  • The Long-Tail and Misguided Benchmarks: Diogo Almeida notes that the AI industry has fallen into the trap of over-promising on flashy demos and outlier benchmarks (like GPQA) rather than building reliable background automation for rote tasks like customer service—which OpenAI has struggled to automate since 2020.

Jev and the Rise of Probabilistic Software

  • A New Software Primitive: Diogo Almeida explains that Jev acts as an intelligent library embedded directly into program state, allowing developers to use natural language combined with state machines to make probabilistic, confident decisions rather than just generating text for humans to interpret.
  • Reliability Over Raw Generative Power: Diogo Almeida emphasizes that achieving deep reliability—focusing on robustness, predictable intelligence, and robustness rather than simple uptime—is the fundamental barrier that enables developers to enter a flow state and program against AI without constant manual example queries.
  • Transitioning from LLM Loops to Internal Guts: Diogo Almeida highlights that previous attempts to embed AI into software resulted in awkward "while loops" (agents talking to LLMs) or human-in-the-loop chat interfaces, whereas Jev aims to push intelligence deep into the "guts" of software infrastructure (comparable to the reliability tiers of TCP/UDP).

The SaaS "Inverse Apocalypse"

  • The Software Capabilities Gap: Ben Horowitz and Diogo Almeida discuss how standard PRs for large companies are often only ~10 lines of code, meaning coding agents only automate superficial edits rather than expanding core software capabilities.
  • SaaS as the Ultimate Beneficiary: Diogo Almeida rejects the "SaaS apocalypse" narrative, arguing that established SaaS companies are best positioned to leverage Jev because they deeply understand user workflows and enterprise needs.
  • Rebuilding Critical Systems: Martin Casado suggests that due to both new probabilistic software capabilities and mounting cybersecurity challenges, critical infrastructure and systems are primed to be entirely rebuilt using these new primitives.

Diogo Almeida’s Unorthodox Journey & Philosophy

  • From Mathlete to Systems Engineer: Diogo Almeida shares his background as an award-winning mathlete who eventually gravitated toward computer science because it was "math, but cool, useful, and fun."
  • Kaggle and Big Lab Experience: Diogo Almeida describes winning a Kaggle competition through brute-force systems automation (which led to speaking at NeurIPS and meeting Isabel Guong), followed by stints working with Jeremy Howard, Google Brain, and OpenAI.
  • Build Prod, Not God: Martin Casado and Diogo Almeida celebrate TypeSafe’s philosophy of "build prod, not god"—rejecting ominous mono-model Doomerism in favor of an optimistic, developer-friendly movement where automation creates better, more rewarding jobs.

Inside the Personal AI Assistant Growing 10% a Day | Instinct Founder

Invest Like The Best(107.0K)·85:27·78.5K views·2026-09-28
Patrick O'ShaughnessyHost, Invest Like The Best Noah ShinnCEO, Instinct
TL;DR- **App-less Architecture:** **Noah Shinn** explains that Instinct has no standalone mobile or desktop application; users interact with it through everyday communication channels like text, email, and voice calls.
Instinct is an app-less, proactive AI personal assistant created by CEO Noah Shinn that is growing 10% day-over-day and already handling over $1 billion a year in transaction volume. Operating via text, voice, and email, the agent functions with high social intelligence...

TL;DR: Instinct is an app-less, proactive AI personal assistant created by CEO Noah Shinn that is growing 10% day-over-day and already handling over $1 billion a year in transaction volume. Operating via text, voice, and email, the agent functions with high social intelligence to autonomously manage complex real-world workflows like travel, reservations, and personal finance.

1. Product Experience & Core Philosophy

  • App-less Architecture: Noah Shinn explains that Instinct has no standalone mobile or desktop application; users interact with it through everyday communication channels like text, email, and voice calls.
  • Focus on Understandability over Raw Capability: Noah Shinn emphasizes prioritizing how well a user understands and predicts the agent's behavior rather than hyping underlying technical features, aiming to reduce cognitive load and user fatigue.
  • Proactive and Autonomous Execution: Unlike traditional prompt-response AI tools, Noah Shinn notes that Instinct operates on higher-level objectives (e.g., booking a last-minute flight to New York via a single voice note, handling entire travel itineraries from Uber to hotel), waking up and sleeping in the background to manage logistics proactively.

2. Growth Metrics & Financial Milestones

  • Explosive Viral Growth: Noah Shinn reports that Instinct is currently growing at 10% to 11% day-over-day entirely through word-of-mouth.
  • Invite-Only Constraint: The platform uses an invite-only model where each user receives 5 invites, creating high social demand and secondary market buzz (with invites reportedly selling for $300 on eBay).
  • High Transaction Volume: Despite a very small user base in an invite-only phase, Noah Shinn reveals the platform is approaching over $1 billion a year in transaction volume, with 50% coming from travel alone.
  • Venture Backing: Noah Shinn mentions the company recently raised a $1 billion round at a roughly $10 billion valuation, led by tier-one investors including Zoya, Benchmark, and Kotu.

3. The Instinct-to-Instinct Network & Social Dynamics

  • Trusted Person Network: Noah Shinn details a feature launched about 10 days prior to the interview that allows different users' Instinct agents to communicate directly to solve collaborative tasks like calendar scheduling without manual back-and-forth.
  • Granular Access Controls: The network relies on strict trust boundaries and customizable sharing levels (e.g., sharing a work calendar or inbox with colleagues versus full access with a spouse).
  • Social Consequences of Trust: Noah Shinn points out fascinating emerging social dynamics where a breach of data privacy by an agent visiting unauthorized areas of a connected user's profile can directly strain the real-world human relationship.

4. Trust, Privacy, and Security Architecture

  • Adoption Milestones as Proxies for Trust: Noah Shinn shares that 3 weeks in, 40% of the user base has shared a personal credit card with Instinct, and users who share at least one sensitive piece of information exhibit an 80% retention rate.
  • Decoupled Security Guardrails: Noah Shinn explains that safety is managed by infrastructure detached from the main agent architecture:
    • Firewalls: Intercept and block malicious text or media inputs attempting prompt injection.
    • Decoupled Watchdog Monitors: Actively monitor, pause, and approve/disapprove every agentic action or thought trace before execution to prevent hallucinations or unauthorized data access.

5. Business Model & Impact on Existing Industries

  • Alignment Over Exploitation: Noah Shinn explicitly rejects ad-supported models that monetize user attention or manipulate consumer behavior against their will, stating that Instinct acts purely on behalf of the user's true interests.
  • Merchant-Paid Take Rates: Modeled after payment networks like Apple Pay or American Express, the product remains free for the consumer while generating revenue through transaction take rates paid by underlying merchants and service providers (leveraging high merchant margins in sectors like travel and boutique hotels).
  • Transformation of Digital Services: Noah Shinn predicts that zero-friction agentic interaction will fundamentally rewrite the internet's business model, increasing total transaction volume for merchants by removing the friction of traditional app interfaces and infinite-scroll attention traps.

"Any product that is dependent on consumer laziness or inertia is toast... Yes. Good for the consumer." — Patrick O'Shaughnessy and Noah Shinn

NEC's Kevin Hassett Talks US Economic Goals | Bloomberg Talks

Bloomberg Podcasts(584.0K)·21:47·781 views·2026-09-28
Kevin HassettNational Economic Director, White House David WestinHost, Bloomberg Television
TL;DR*"Think about this.
White House National Economic Director Kevin Hassett expressed strong optimism for U.S. economic growth, projecting GDP expansion around 4% driven by capital spending, labor supply increases, and an AI-fueled productivity boom, while acknowledging that fiscal deficit...

TL;DR: White House National Economic Director Kevin Hassett expressed strong optimism for U.S. economic growth, projecting GDP expansion around 4% driven by capital spending, labor supply increases, and an AI-fueled productivity boom, while acknowledging that fiscal deficit targets will depend on outside forces and policy trade-offs.

Economic Growth & Supply-Side Drivers

  • Current Growth Metrics:
    • Real GDP: Currently tracking in the 5% range (specifically cited at 5.014 real), supported by strong incoming economic data.
    • Growth Projections: Base case growth is expected to cruise around 4% (rather than 3%), assuming no disruptions from the outside world.
  • Growth Accounting Breakdown:
    • Productivity Growth: Estimated at approximately 2.5%.
    • Capital Deepening: Estimated at 0.7% to 0.8%.
    • Labor Supply: Estimated to contribute roughly similarly to capital deepening.
  • Labor Market Indicators:
    • Weekly Wages: The administration emphasizes average weekly wages over hourly wages because tax stimuli are driving longer work weeks and increased work hours.
    • Unemployment Claims: Initial claims for unemployment insurance are at their lowest levels since World War II, showing minimal labor market stress.

Artificial Intelligence & Productivity

  • AI Productivity Boom:
    • The current productivity boom is being observed in real time *before* benchmark revisions, signaling exceptionally strong momentum.
    • AI subscription prices have remained stable despite massive quality improvements, reflecting unmeasured deflationary productivity gains similar to the 1990s internet boom.
  • Community Pushback:
    • Local communities and states (such as Texas and New York) are enacting data center moratoriums.
    • Voluntary Framework: The administration’s AI policy remains strictly voluntary—no town is forced to host a data center, but firms building them must generate their own power.
  • Labor Impact of AI:
    • Academic studies show that firms adopting AI experience significant increases in sales, employment, and wages.
    • Conversely, the primary labor market stress is concentrated in firms that fail to adopt AI and consequently struggle or fail.

Fiscal Policy, Deficits, & National Net Worth

  • Federal Workforce Reductions:
    • Federal employment has been reduced by approximately 300,000 workers, yielding roughly $500 billion in budget savings over 10 years.
    • Displaced workers transitioning to the private sector earn on average about $30,000 more than in government work, generating an additional $100 billion in tax benefits.
    • DC-area job numbers show no negative disruption from these cuts, as workers either retired or quickly secured private sector employment.
  • Deficit and Debt Perspectives:
    • Reaching a 3% of GDP deficit target by the end of the term depends on avoiding unforeseen "force majeure" measures, though deficit reduction remains a priority for the president and key members of Congress.
    • Household Net Worth: While government debt is a liability for future generations, external debt sits at around $15 trillion, whereas household net worth net of debt has surged by roughly $90 trillion over the past decade.
    • Capital vs. Consumption: Federal policy has focused on building future capital (via tax and capital formation reforms) rather than just mailing consumption checks, resulting in historic capital formation numbers.

Interest Rates & Yields

  • Real Return on Capital:
    • Rising long-term bond yields (including a 3% real yield on 30-year TIPS with 2.1% inflation) reflect a higher real return on capital driven by deregulation and pro-capital tax policies.
    • General equilibrium dynamics mean that high returns on capital (e.g., borrowing at 6% to fund a data center yielding 20%) naturally push borrowing rates upward until markets clear.

Trumpanomics vs. Prior Economic Frameworks

  • Pillars of Trumpanomics:
    • Worker-First Supply Side: Features worker-centric tax policies (such as no tax on tips and average tax refunds near $3,500 after major tax legislation) alongside traditional Reagan-style business supply-side incentives and faster deregulation.
    • Trade Policy: Aggressive use of tariffs is driving a massive wave of domestic onshoring.
  • Deficit Trade-Offs:
    • Similar to Reaganomics, upfront tax reforms and pro-growth policies can initially expand deficits, but the long-term benefit of a high-capital, high-growth economy justifies the policy bundle.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Kevin Hassett predicts the U.S. economy will "cruise around four [percent] instead of three" for growth, driven by capital spending, labor supply increases, and an AI productivity boom.
  • Kevin Hassett claims that the deficit will track toward a 3% of GDP target over the next two years, though achievement will depend on whether "force majeure measures" arise.

Notable Quotes

*"Think about this. If we're having a productivity boom and we're measuring it before the benchmark revision, we're seeing it in real time. So, how good's the benchmark revision going to be? And so, I'd say that we're really, really bullish on the momentum that we have going forward."* — Kevin Hassett
*"If you look at the liability for future generations, it's actually smaller than you might think when we're looking at, US net numbers because, you know, a lot of, uh, debt is debt we owe ourselves."* — Kevin Hassett
*"Suppose that you could borrow right now at 6% and buy a data center and make 20%. ... And when you do that as much as you can, then the six goes up and the 20 goes down."* — Kevin Hassett

I'll tell you an interesting story about the internet because I was a senior economist at the Federal Reserve in Washington with Alan Greenspan when the internet was just starting. I was in charge of researching and forecasting the business sector. One of the things the government agencies started to do was hedonic adjustment of the computer deflator. You went from like an 80286 to a Pentium and it cost about the same, but the Pentium was 10 times better, and they would show that up as a decline in the price of computing because for the same dollar you get way more computing. At one point, Alan came into my office, or sent a note, and I went to his office, and he said, "You know, something doesn't make sense because telecommunications equipment is basically all computers now. I called the Bureau of Economic Analysis and said, "What's going on?" They said, "We have a model way that we calculate price changes and we don't take things out of the bundle where we measure the price changes until the thing is no longer made in the US." They were still making telecoms equipment you could see on the shelf and shipping it to developing countries who had old analog things. Since US firms were still making it, it was still in the bundle that was counted in the deflator. I did a memo for Greenspan that’s presumably viable now where I said if we just were to take the communications deflator and treat it as if it's the computer deflator, GDP grows like 1.5% higher than measured back then. The thing that's interesting to me about this productivity boom is that you're not seeing any of the hedonic action that one would expect that we saw back then in the '90s. I love doing this when I'm lecturing undergraduates and I'll say, "Suppose you want to have higher consumption in the future. How do you do that?" So if you have a really low return on capital, you'll have a low interest rate. If you have a really high return on capital, you have a higher interest rate. TL;DR

President Trump announced a historic $18 billion investment by Mesabi Metallics (SR Group) to build the largest steel plant in U.S. history in southeastern Iowa, backed by a related $2.5 billion iron ore mine in Minnesota. The project aims to create thousands of jobs and establish a fully integrated domestic supply chain.

Steel Plant & Mining Investment

  • Iowa Steel Complex: Mesabi Metallics will build an integrated steel plant in southeastern Iowa, representing an estimated $18 billion investment.
  • Production Scale & Timing: The facility will produce 10 million tons of steel per year using advanced technology, competing globally on size, quality, and cost. Construction is slated to start immediately.
  • Minnesota Iron Ore Mine: Backed by the Export-Import Bank, a $2.5 billion project opened the first new iron ore mine in the U.S. in over 50 years on the Minnesota iron range, producing high-grade "Patriot pellets" to supply the Iowa mill.
  • Supply Chain Integration: The project creates a fully domestic supply chain: ore mined in Minnesota, transported on American railroads, and refined into steel in Iowa using American energy.

Economic Impact & Job Creation

  • Job Projections:
    • Construction: Up to 6,000 new construction jobs.
    • Operations: Nearly 2,000 manufacturing and mining jobs (including 1,700 permanent jobs at the Iowa plant).
    • Salary Impact: Average salaries around $49,000, enabling family incomes over $100,000.
  • Economic Boost: Expected to add $95 billion to the U.S. economy, accompanied by infrastructure enhancements including natural gas pipelines, power, rail, and roads in a previously underserved region.
  • Broader Re-industrialization: Officials cited $47 billion in ongoing or announced steel sector projects, nearly 60,000 new manufacturing jobs created in the current year, and a milestone where U.S. steel production surpassed Japan's for the first time in 26 years.

Administration Policy & Tariff Impact

  • Section 232 Tariffs: Officials credited the administration's 50% foreign steel tariffs as the decisive catalyst that made the domestic mine and plant investments financially viable.
  • National Security: Speakers emphasized domestic steel production as critical for defense equipment, such as army tanks and aircraft, removing reliance on foreign suppliers like Brazil and China.

Trade Relations (Canada & International)

  • Canada Trade Friction: President Trump criticized Canadian trade policies, citing 400%+ dairy tariffs and unfair treatment of U.S. farmers, predicting Canada will seek a fairer trade deal within three to four weeks.
  • Global Sourcing: While most critical materials will be sourced domestically, high-quality steel-refining additives like manganese may still be globally sourced, including potential imports from Africa.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Construction Start: Construction on the Iowa steel plant will begin "immediately" — President Donald Trump.
  • Inflation Outlook: Inflation will be "eradicated totally" as soon as the war in Iran ends — President Donald Trump.
  • Canada Trade Deal: Canada will come to the table within "three or four weeks" to eliminate high tariffs and negotiate a fair trade deal — President Donald Trump.

The Defender's Window: Cyber security keynote

OpenAI(2.1M)·47:24·24.6K views·2026-09-28
Matt KnightHead of Security, OpenAI
TL;DROpenAI security experts introduced the concept of the "defenders' window" and their "defense factory" framework, urging organizations to immediately leverage frontier AI models (such as GPT-6 Astra and Soul) and tools like CodeX Security to find and ...
patch vulnerabilities before...

TL;DR

OpenAI security experts introduced the concept of the "defenders' window" and their "defense factory" framework, urging organizations to immediately leverage frontier AI models (such as GPT-6 Astra and Soul) and tools like CodeX Security to find and patch vulnerabilities before attackers exploit them.

Context & Ecosystem Momentum

  • Rapid AI Adoption: Emanuel Mariel noted massive weekly active user growth, including 26% more for marketing and 108x more for legal using Codex. He cited an SMB named Stadler (650 employees) achieving 30-40% efficiency gains using 145 agents.
  • Global Security Concerns: Cyber security is now the top topic in customer conversations. European and international agencies—such as the EU cyber agency ENISA and Ukraine (facing 6,000 attacks in the last year)—are actively utilizing models to find vulnerabilities.
  • Open Letter & Funding: OpenAI published an open letter signed by 500 organizations calling for a collective response, alongside a $1 billion fund to subsidize Daybreak access and help critical infrastructure, essential services, nonprofits, and open source maintainers fix vulnerabilities.

Frontier Model Capabilities & Safety (GPT-6 Astra & Soul)

  • Performance Benchmarks: Lena introduced GPT-6 Astra (OpenAI's most intelligent and aligned model yet), noting its strength in computer use, zero-day discovery, and red teaming. On the *exploit gym* evaluation (turning known vulnerabilities into working exploits across Chrome's JavaScript engine and the Linux kernel), GPT-5.6 Soul reached ~30% completion, whereas Astra reached ~40% completion using far fewer tokens.
  • Exploit Chain Discovery: Researchers using OpenAI models uncovered two previously unknown vulnerabilities in Chrome's JavaScript engine and connected them into an exploit chain, which Google subsequently fixed.
  • Patch the Planet Initiative: Partnered with Trail of Bits to secure open-source projects (Python, curl, go). In the first week alone, 37 patches were merged, and AIO_HTTP maintainers fixed 8 issues within hours.
  • Safety & Alignment Layers: Astra reached the cyber-critical threshold, prompting multiple protection layers (refusing harmful requests, detecting abuse, restricting high-risk accounts, monitoring reasoning). In out-of-scope exploit tests, Soul successfully exploited targets 48% of the time, while Astra achieved 0% successful exploits.

Daybreak Tiers & CodeX Security Workflows

  • Daybreak Access Tiers:
    • Daybreak Red: Highest level of cyber access for approved security teams to conduct advanced red teaming and weaponized exploit development.
    • Daybreak Blue: General-purpose frontier models (Soul, Luna, and soon Astra) tailored for code review, alert investigation, vulnerability finding, and patch creation.
  • CodeX Security Red: Vanessa demonstrated CodeX Security using Ladybird (a trending pre-alpha open-source browser on GitHub). Features include:
    • Full Scans & Context Seeding: Scanning repositories (e.g., 28,000 files reviewed for Ladybird) using high reasoning models, seeded with enterprise context, threat vectors, and a `security.md` file at the repo level.
    • Automated Remediation: Invoking the `fix finding` skill to generate, review, locally apply, and verify patches.
    • Ecosystem Extensibility: Using sub-agents to group findings, open Jira tickets, draft GitHub PRs, and notify engineers via Slack.
    • CLI and SDK for Scale: Bulk scanning capabilities via `repositories.csv` to scale across thousands of repositories, alongside CI/CD pipeline checks.

The Internal Defense Factory & Continuous Defense

  • Internal Mobilization: Lou detailed OpenAI's internal code red, mobilizing 250 cross-functional people across engineering, security, and research to build a defense factory.
  • Factory Architecture: Runs inside a network using virtual machines, Microsoft's open-standard dev containers for reproducibility and tool hooks (e.g., Falcon sensors, Wiz monitoring), and agents with organization-specific skills.
  • Performance Metrics: OpenAI's internal defense factory achieved:
    • False positive rate: < 1% (down from traditional 50-60%)
    • Ownership assignment success: ~90%
    • Remediation fix roll-back rate: < 1%
  • Productization: OpenAI is actively dogfooding this architecture and rolling out early versions to a small handful of customers, with broader product rollouts coming in subsequent weeks and months.

Notable Quotes

"Security is an essential essential element of trust that's for us on OpenAI... Trust comes also with what we do as a team as an ecosystem on this security piece." — Emanuel Mariel
"The same capabilities that gave us these weaknesses also make it easier for attackers to exploit them. We can't assume we're going to stay ahead, but we do know we're ahead for now." — Matt
"We shouldn't be taking risks on behalf of humanity. People need to remain in control." — Lena (referencing Sam Altman's philosophy)
"Don't wait for us. All the pieces are here. We don't have time." — Matt

My name is Lee, and I lead our cyber program across EMEA for go-to-market. I've spent the last six months partnering with Europe's largest and most critical organizations to improve their defensive capabilities. Today, I'm going to walk you through how we built the best and the safest cybersecurity models and how we can make these available to you. At the beginning of the month, we launched GPT6 Astra, our most intelligent model yet. It's a huge step forward for those everyday tasks, including software engineering and professional work, and it's particularly good at computer use. That means you can hand off a lot of the admin that you normally do yourself and let the model work through it on your computer. But it's also a huge leap forward with cyber. We've trained it to find more vulnerabilities, including zero-days, and to dig deeper into security problems and be a more capable red teamer. But capability is only part of the story. We also need to keep making that intelligence more cost-efficient so that people can afford to put it to work at scale. To see how we're progressing on both of these fronts, we use a range of evaluations. One way that we measure that is with "exploit gym." It tests whether a model can turn a known software vulnerability into a working exploit across applications like Chrome's JavaScript engine and the Linux kernel. The model has to get the exploit working to score. Just triggering a crash isn't enough. Along the bottom, you can see the number of output tokens that are used, and then up the side, the percentage of challenges successfully completed. On the graph here, you can see the output for GPT-5.6, where it reached around 30% completion. Now, if you take a look at Astra, it reached around 40% more successful completions, but it used far fewer tokens to do it. And that's the progress that we want to highlight: tackling more of these difficult tasks with much less output needed to get there. So for defenders, that opens up the possibility of doing more thorough security testing with the resources that they have. So, what does this look like in practice? Working with our researchers, our models helped uncover two previously unknown vulnerabilities in Chrome's JavaScript engine and connect them into an exploit chain. One flaw allowed a memory corruption and a second let that corruption break through another layer of protection. The hard part was understanding how these weaknesses work together. Our researchers validated these findings and reported them to Google, who then released a fix. So this is just one concrete example of these tools already helping to make software that we use every day much safer. And we're taking that work beyond individual examples. "Patch the Planet" is our initiative with Trail of Bits to help secure widely used open-source software, including projects like Python, curl, and Go. We fund expert security researchers and give them access to our models and Codeex security, and they work directly with the maintainers to investigate the findings, develop and test patches, and coordinate disclosure. In the first week alone, 37 patches were merged, and in one of those projects, which was AIOHTTP, maintainers fixed eight reported issues within hours. It's real progress for these small teams maintaining this software that we all depend on. But the same capabilities that help defenders can also be misused. As models become more capable, our safeguards have to advance with them. And Astra is our first model to reach the cyber-critical threshold that brings real opportunities for the defenders and a responsibility to manage risks carefully. As Sam has said previously, we shouldn't be taking risks on behalf of humanity. People need to remain in control. And to do that with our cyber models, we use several layers of protection, including training the models to refuse harmful requests, detecting and blocking abuse, and monitoring for dangerous behavior. Together, these help us put powerful tools in defenders' hands while making them harder to misuse. And with Astra, we strengthened those layers with better detection for cyber misuse and attempts to bypass the safeguards. It also included tighter restrictions for higher-risk accounts and monitors that examine the model's reasoning and actions. These help us spot when a model goes outside its instructions and allows us to intervene. And that responsibility extends to what we'll build next. Safety needs to stay ahead of the capability. And that's why we've previously paused frontier training while we double down on monitoring and alignment research. We need to meet the required safety thresholds before we push the capability further. But alongside those safeguards, Astra is our most aligned model yet. Here's one way that we test that. We give the model a difficult task which includes an out-of-scope target that the model could exploit as a shortcut. Without these production safeguards, Soul successfully exploited that target in around 48% of cases. But with Astra, we saw zero of those successful exploits for that test. That matters because we want the models to respect the boundaries that we set even when the task gets really hard, and it's meaningful progress to make sure that we can delegate more complex work with greater confidence while keeping people in control. So next, I wanted to talk about how we actually build these capabilities. We start with the coding and reasoning skills in our frontier models and then we go and add specialist cyber training on top of that, including understanding vulnerabilities, developing working exploits, and verifying the results. We make these capabilities available in two Daybreak tiers: Red and Blue. And Daybreak Red is our highest level of cyber access, and it gives approved security teams access to these specialist models for advanced red teaming and weaponized exploit development. Think taking a crash and then turning it into a working exploit or chaining weaknesses together to see how far an attacker could get. But giving an agent this level of capability also means we need to be clear about its boundaries. We need to include information about what it's allowed to test, what it can reach, and then also allow your team to see what it's doing. Today, teams often have to build all of this supporting infrastructure themselves: the execution environment, the network controls, monitoring, and evidence collection—and it takes time away from their security work. So today, we're announcing Codeex Security Red, a managed way to run penetration tests against your applications with all of the scope, controls, and oversight built directly into the workflow. So here's how that's going to work. You'll start in Codeex, and you'll define the assessment scope and the rules of engagement, and then the investigation agents run in an OpenAI-hosted environment, each with its own isolated sandbox. Your team follows their activity through the dashboard where findings and supporting evidence can come together to be reviewed. Now, when those agents interact with your own application, their outgoing requests pass through network controls, with a guardian agent reviewing the traffic. And that allows us to connect the investigation to your environment with controls on what the agents can reach and what they're able to do. So this allows you to test what an attacker could actually achieve while keeping visibility and full control over the whole assessment. So for your team, that starts with less infrastructure to build and maintain. We're bringing the execution environment, monitoring, and findings together all into one view, so you can spend more time investigating these weaknesses and getting them fixed. It also brings safeguards into the workflow with isolated environments and checks on outgoing requests that help manage the risks that come with using models that are this capable. It also gives your team more capacity to test applications and investigate potential attack paths. The aim is to make thorough, repeatable assessments easier to run with the team that you already have. And finally, it gives you the controls to feel confident in how you run the model. You can set the scope of what the model can access and you can block and restrict activity at the network level. And then next, for most defenders, the starting point is Daybreak Blue. It gives you our frontier general-purpose models with safeguards that are tailored to allow authorized security work. Think reviewing code, investigating alerts, finding vulnerabilities, and then creating the patches. It helps your teams find new issues and work through the vulnerability backlog. The Secret Behind Wall Street’s Biggest Brands

Sourcery with Molly O'Shea(73.7K)·65:23·4.2K views·2026-09-28
Jen ProsekFounder and Managing Partner, Prosek Partners Molly O'SheaHost, Sourcery
TL;DRJen Prosek, Founder & Managing Partner of Prosek Partners, explains how the world's most powerful financial and technology institutions build proactive brands, manage high-stakes crises, and navigate reputation management in the age of LLMs and AI.
The Digital Blink: Jen...

TL;DR

Jen Prosek, Founder & Managing Partner of Prosek Partners, explains how the world's most powerful financial and technology institutions build proactive brands, manage high-stakes crises, and navigate reputation management in the age of LLMs and AI.

Reputation Management & The "Digital Blink"

  • The Digital Blink: Jen Prosek warns that people and LLMs size up an individual or firm in a fraction of a second based on online presence—making digital hygiene critical since "if you don't feed the machine positive content, the machine just grabs what's available."
  • Controlling the Narrative in Crises: When faced with negative stories, leaders must assess whether speaking out will add fuel to the fire, but Prosek emphasizes that leaders should *never* allow an untrue reputation to calcify around them.
  • Building the Internal/External Muscle: Consistent momentum content, third-party media placement, and direct-to-audience social channels help maintain brand health before bad news strikes.

Financial Brands & The Evolution of Offense

  • From Defense to Offense: Historically viewed as a defensive function called only during crises, branding in finance shifted post-2006/2007 financial crisis when firms like Goldman Sachs recognized the need for offensive brand building.
  • Industry Spending Benchmarks: Prosek notes that smaller firms (under $2B AUM) typically spend $250k or less on marketing/brand, while complex global and multi-product institutional firms spend anywhere from $500k to $4M, with mega-budgets stretching up to $10M for retail-focused campaigns like F1 and US Open sponsorships.
  • The Talent, Deals, and Capital Rubric: Prosek converts skeptical founders by proving that a strong brand drives better employee recruitment, superior deal sourcing, and more efficient fundraising.

Case Studies: TradFi and Private Markets

  • Apollo: Mark Rowan successfully resurrected Apollo's brand and culture following the Leon Black era by diversifying the asset manager, embracing accessibility, and shifting from a "black box" mystique to an approachable, multi-product juggernaut.
  • Citadel: Ken Griffin transformed Citadel from a high-intensity, "quant-trading gulag" reputation into a respected powerhouse by actively engaging in policy, politics, and transparent social channels.
  • Blackstone: An early adopter of institutional marketing, Blackstone leveraged smart retail campaigns (such as John Gray's running videos on LinkedIn) to capture retail and high-net-worth audiences efficiently.

Silicon Valley, AI, and Founder Personal Brands

  • The AI Narrative Problem: Prosek notes that tech ecosystems have struggled with mainstream and political ecosystems due to Tone-deaf messaging around job cuts and societal impacts.
  • Jensen Huang's Ambassador Role: NVIDIA CEO Jensen Huang successfully broke out of Silicon Valley by acting as an ambassador for AI, engaging Wall Street, Washington, Europe, and Asia to position AI around American competitiveness and job creation.
  • Meta & Mark Zuckerberg: Meta effectively navigated corporate accountability for social media harm by running ads framing safety as an industry-wide challenge rather than a single-company problem, though Mark Zuckerberg's personal brand continues to face historical baggage.

Advisory, EQ, and Content Strategy

  • The Chief EQ Officer Role: Prosek describes her firm's role as being the "truth teller" at the table, checking powerful leaders on low-EQ moves and ensuring they do not alienate internal employees—who account for over 50–75% of negative leaks.
  • Podcast & Long-Form ROI: Prosek highlights the immense value of long-form audio assets, noting she has generated $17 million in fees from a single podcast appearance (Ted Seides' *Capital Allocators*).
  • Venture Capital Strategy: As VC firms increasingly mimic media companies (e.g., Andreessen Horowitz) or cultivate quiet luxury (e.g., Thrive), Prosek advises founders to avoid overexposure and cringe content, focusing instead on genuine partnership and substantive insights.

Notable Quotes

  • > "I had this cockamamey idea that we were going to be the firm that [treated brand as an offense game]... We were selling something no one wanted to buy for years." — Jen Prosek
  • > "The higher you go up the food chain, the less people are telling you the truth. So you need somebody to not tell you, 'Oh, you're amazing, you were so great... You need the person that kind of tells you what you need to improve." — Jen Prosek
  • > "If you're not feeding the machine a little, you're not controlling anything. So if something bad happens, what's out there? Only the bad stuff." — Jen Prosek

What the AI Boom Looks Like From Inside Cisco with Sam Badri | The Real Eisman Playbook Ep 77

Steve Eisman(205.0K)·43:50·82.2K views·2026-09-28
Steve EismanHost, The Real Eisman Playbook Sam BadriHead of Investor Relations, Cisco
TL;DRSam Badri, Head of Investor Relations at Cisco, explains that Cisco's revenue growth has accelerated from normalized mid-single digits to 15-18%, driven by a booming hyperscaler AI business and a campus refresh cycle.
He believes the AI boom is still in its early innings, noting...

TL;DR

Sam Badri, Head of Investor Relations at Cisco, explains that Cisco's revenue growth has accelerated from normalized mid-single digits to 15-18%, driven by a booming hyperscaler AI business and a campus refresh cycle. He believes the AI boom is still in its early innings, noting that roughly 90% of users remain stuck at the basic prompt stage rather than using advanced skills files or AI agents.

Cisco's Revenue Acceleration & Growth Drivers

  • Normalized Growth Baseline: When Sam Badri joined Cisco three years ago (2023), the company's normalized revenue growth was mid-single digits (around 5%), which the sell-side viewed with extreme skepticism.
  • Recent Revenue Acceleration: Cisco's most recent quarter saw revenue growth of 18%, with fiscal year 2027 guidance set at 15% growth at the midpoint.
  • Hyperscaler Business Expansion: The hyperscaler cloud business is experiencing significant momentum, with revenue doubling in FY27 while remaining below 20% of the total company.
  • Hyperscaler Product Mix: Cisco sells high-performance switches designed for hyperscale data center chips and coherent pluggables (optical/Ethernet connection products).
  • Campus Refresh Cycle: Representing a major portion of growth alongside AI, Cisco is executing a "rip and replace" cycle as enterprise customers replace older gear that has been deployed for 15+ years and is reaching end-of-support.
  • Operational Milestones: In fiscal year 2026, Cisco reported record revenue, record operating margin, and record earnings per employee.

Cisco vs. Arista & Network Infrastructure

  • Silicon Strategy Differentiation: Cisco designs and manufactures its own proprietary silicon (such as the G300 NPU/network processing unit announced in February 2026), whereas competitor Arista primarily purchases third-party silicon and focuses on advanced software engineering.
  • Growth Disparity: Arista has historically grown faster than Cisco because its revenue mix is heavily indexed to hyperscalers, whereas Cisco maintains large legacy businesses (security, collaboration, services).

The State of AI Adoption & Token Consumption

  • The 90% Rule: Approximately 90% of current AI users are stuck at the beginner stage using simple prompts, with very few having progressed to advanced usage like skills files or AI agents.
  • Skills Files & Productivity: Power users build customized skills files (e.g., Sam Badri's 40-page word doc of valuation and research instructions) to dramatically accelerate institutional research and workflow productivity.
  • Model Distribution: Enterprises use a mix of open-weight models (for cost-efficient, standard tasks) and frontier models (like Anthropic and OpenAI for intensive, high-stakes tasks requiring top-tier intelligence).
  • Durability of Demand: The health of the AI infrastructure trade hinges on the collective demand acceleration for tokens by consumers of frontier models.

Data Center Constraints & Engineering Complexity

  • Texas Power Audits: Even in data center-friendly states like Texas, rapid buildouts have led to grid allocation audits and a massive backlog queue to vet real capacity versus double-booked demand.
  • Scale and Density Shifts: Data center facilities have scaled from historical large projects of 100-200 megawatts (circa 2021) to gigawatt-scale operations today.
  • Power Density Exploded: Rack-level power density has surged from 35-40 kilowatts per rack up to 150-650 kilowatts, requiring advanced electrical architectures like 800-volt direct current.
  • Water Efficiency Metric: The water usage equivalent of 38,000 AI prompts equals the water required to produce a single almond.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Fiscal Year 2027 Revenue Guidance: Sam Badri states that Cisco's FY27 guidance is 15% revenue growth at the midpoint.
  • Hyperscaler Revenue Growth: Sam Badri states that Cisco's hyperscale business with clouds is "almost doubling in FY27."

Notable Quotes

*"When we guided to 5% topline growth for essentially kind of a multi-year long-term guide, the sellside analyst reports essentially described this guidance as... 'here's how we think this growth could be achievable.' It was a very glass half empty type of framing."* — Sam Badri
*"If you are going to use these tools and you're going to use them in the right way that equals a lot more token usage hence the greater activity that you might see in all these frontier companies."* — Sam Badri
*"The company's been around for 40 years plus. So, you know, in a very short period of time we've evolved quite dramatically and we've introduced new products and we've prepared accordingly."* — Sam Badri
